EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Special Analyses are performed to evaluate the significance of new information or new 
analytical methods to the results and associated conclusions of a performance assessment (PA).  
As waste tanks and ancillary equipment are cleaned at the F-Tank Farm (FTF) at the U.S. Depart 
of Energy’s (DOE) Savannah River Site (SRS), final residual inventories will be used to update 
the FTF fate and transport modeling performed as part of SRS-REG-2007-00002, Revision 1 
(hereinafter referred to as FTF PA).  This allows for evaluation of the difference between the 
projected and final waste tank inventories to determine if the results of the FTF PA, and the 
conclusions reached based on the FTF PA information remain valid.   The Tank 18/Tank 19 
Special Analysis for the Performance Assessment for the F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River 
Site (SRR-CWDA-2010-00124), (hereinafter referred to as the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis) 
evaluated the impact of the final Tank 18 and 19 inventories and therefore represents the updated 
FTF base case dose results.  The Tank 5 and 6 Special Analysis uses the FTF PA Base Case 
(Case A) model to evaluate the final residuals that are planned to be grouted in-place in Tanks 5 
and 6 (using final residual characterization data).  This Special Analysis progresses from the 
Tank 18/19 Special Analysis and takes advantage of new information gathered and generated 
since both the FTF PA and Tank 18/19 Special Analysis were developed.  This includes 
information used to address some of the observations and recommendations found in the U.S. 
Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) Technical Evaluation Report for F-Area Tank Farm 
Facility (ML112371715).  The issuance of the Technical Evaluation Report (TER) culminated a 
multi-year consultation with NRC under the Ronald W. Reagan National Defense Authorization 
Act for Fiscal Year 2005 (NDAA) Section 3116.  In particular, sensitivity studies were carried out 
in response to questions regarding waste-release solubility modeling, natural attenuation in soil, 
and groundwater conditioning impacts. 
The results of this Special Analysis demonstrate that the conclusions reached based on the FTF 
PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis remain valid.  In addition, sensitivity analyses (using 
lessons learned from waste-release modeling since the FTF PA was issued) shows that peak 
dose, beyond 10,000 years after FTF closure, is more attenuated and occurs later in time than 
projected in the FTF PA Base Case.  Further, the special analysis process has confirmed that 
there continues to be reasonable assurance that the Title 10 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) 
Part 61 Subpart C performance objectives will be met for FTF during the reasonably foreseeable 
future.1  A comparison was made of the peak all-pathways dose within 10,000 years, calculated 
using projected FTF waste tank inventories plus actual residual inventories for those FTF waste 
tanks that have been cleaned and characterized.  As shown in Figure ES-1, the maximum peak 
annual all-pathways dose to a member of the public (MOP) for 10,000 years following FTF 
closure using the updated inventories (including actual Tank 5 and 6 inventories) is 
                                                 
1 In accordance with DOE Manual 435.1-1, Chapter 4, the performance assessment considers a period of 1,000 years 
after the disposal facility has been closed, to assess compliance with the performance objectives.  The Manual 
concludes that longer times of assessment are not used to assess compliance because of the inherently large 
uncertainties in extrapolating such calculations over long periods of time.  Nevertheless, DOE considers results for 
time periods much longer than this in making risk-informed decisions related to the stabilization of waste tanks and 
ancillary structures.

approximately 3 mrem/yr and occurs at year 10,000.  This maximum peak all-pathways dose in 
10,000 years is essentially unchanged from the peak dose calculated in the Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis (SRR-CWDA-2010-00124).

Figure ES-2(I) presents the doses to 100,000 years after FTF closure and demonstrates that the 
long-term doses remain less than the 620 mrem/yr average annual dose received by the average 
Unites States resident.  [NCRP-160]  The expanded scale in Figure ES-2(II) provides additional 
detail.  These figures include the results of a Composite Sensitivity Study that was performed to 
evaluate the impact of an improved waste release model and updated radionuclide-specific 
transport values such as Kds.  The resulting peak dose remained below 25 mrem/yr out to 
100,000 years.  The Composite Sensitivity Study uses more probable and defensible inputs,

Page 14 of 160 
The maximum peak annual dose to a FTF intruder is 70 millirem at year 100 following FTF 
closure from a chronic scenario (i.e., drilling through a transfer line and using groundwater 
maximum concentrations at 1 meter from the FTF).  The 70-millirem peak intruder annual dose 
value is essentially unchanged from the FTF PA.   
Peak groundwater-radionuclide concentrations were also calculated using the FTF inventories 
and no maximum contaminant levels (MCLs) or preliminary remediation goals (PRGs) were 
exceeded at 100 meters from the FTF boundary within 1,000 years.  All radionuclides were well 
below the MCL or PRG at the seepline.  The peak concentrations for the chemicals of concern 
were also calculated, and all were less than the MCL or regional screening level (RSL) at a 
distance of 100 meters from the FTF boundary.  These peak chemical concentration results are 
unchanged from the FTF PA.   
In the NRC TER for FTF, ML112371715, the NRC stated: 
“However, considering the fact that unacceptably high peak doses could occur within the 
10,000 year period of compliance with only a factor of 3 or 4 faster time to collective 
failure of a combination of barriers for Tc or Pu, respectively, and considering the large 
uncertainty associated with predictions of long-term performance of engineered barriers, 
NRC staff are not convinced that the high peak doses currently presented in DOE’s PA 
(or lower peak doses of unknown magnitude that might be associated with a more 
realistic model) could not be realized within a 10,000 year compliance period.” 
The peak dose of NRC concern relating to Tc-99 was an approximately 600 millirem annual 
dose occurring approximately 30,000 years following the FTF closure.  This peak dose was 
associated with the estimated residual Tc-99 inventory in the Type I tanks.  Based on the revised 
FTF Tc-99 inventory projections (informed by actual Tank 5 and Tank 6 Tc-99 inventory data) 
this concern is alleviated, as illustrated in Figure ES-2.  The peak dose associated with the 
residual Tc-99 inventories in the Type I tanks is significantly lowered based on factoring in 
actual waste tank data that is now available for the FTF Type I waste tanks.  Concerns regarding 
the peak dose associated with Pu-239 from Tank 18 have been addressed previously in SRR-
CWDA-2010-00124. 
In addition to evaluating the impact of the updated tank inventory data (including final 
inventories for Tanks 5, 6, 17, 18, 19 and 20) on peak doses within 10,000 years following FTF 
closure, additional sensitivity analyses were performed.  The results of these sensitivity analyses 
are used to assess the potential dose impacts of the final inventory, including evaluating out to 
100,000 years after closure for the purposes of “chasing the peaks,” to further understand system 
performance, and inform the closure decision process.  These additional sensitivity analyses 
placed emphasis on understanding releases associated with the residual inventory remaining in 
Tanks 5 and 6 at closure.   
Tanks 5 and 6 are Type I tanks, whose liners are projected in the FTF PA Base Case to remain 
intact until approximately 13,000 years after closure; contaminants are not expected to be 
released until the waste tank liners fail.  Additionally, the chemical and physical properties of 
Nb-93m (a decay product from Zr-93) and Pu-239 act as barriers to release, preventing the dose 
peaks associated with Tanks 5 and 6 from occurring earlier or with an increased magnitude.  
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the 
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 15 of 160 
Initial inventory estimates in the Base Case (for the FTF PA) indicated that Nb-93m would not 
be a significant dose contributor, such that conservative assumptions regarding Nb-93m were 
appropriate.  For example, soil acts as a natural retardant to Nb-93m transport; however, the Base 
Case conservatively assumes that Nb-93m transport will not be retarded by soil. 
Using the revised inventory estimates based on Tank 5 and Tank 6 sampling, however, shows 
that more realistic and probable modeling assumptions are needed to achieve an appropriate 
understanding of the behavior of this contaminant.  A Composite Sensitivity Study (described in 
Section 6.3.6.3) demonstrates that using more appropriate modeling parameters for modeling 
transport (such as soil retardation and solubility controls) reduces the magnitude of Nb-93m 
doses.  Studies regarding the release and transport of Pu-239 are discussed at length in SRR-
CWDA-2010-00124. 
Based on the above discussion, the Special Analysis results continue to provide reasonable 
assurance that compliance is maintained with the specific requirements of 10 CFR 61 as 
referenced in NDAA Section 3116, DOE Manual 435.1-1 (DOE M 435.1-1), and the MCLs.  
The conclusions that were made based on the FTF PA regarding the stabilization of Tanks 5 and 
6 have not been changed by the new information regarding the final residual inventories that are 
planned to be grouted in-place in Tanks 5 and 6.    
1.0 
SPECIAL ANALYSIS PURPOSE 
The purpose of this Special Analysis is to evaluate the information regarding the final residual 
inventories that are planned to be grouted in-place in Tanks 5 and 6.  This new inventory 
information was used to update the FTF fate and transport modeling performed in the FTF PA 
and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  The potential impacts of the new inventory information on 
the assumptions from the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis were also considered.  
Because the FTF PA and Tank 18/19 Special Analysis analyzed projected inventories for Tanks 
5 and 6, this report focuses on the impact of the final residual waste data on the information 
previously presented.  Because there is the possibility of a significant dose associated with some 
Tanks 5 and 6 radionuclides (e.g., Nb-93m and Pu-239) occurring beyond 10,000 years, 
sensitivity analyses were performed as part of this Special Analysis.  These analyses demonstrate 
that the FTF Base Case model incorporates conservative approaches/inputs.  Based on lessons 
learned regarding waste release modeling and distribution coefficients, projected peak doses will 
be significantly lower than the Base Case modeling results.   
It is not intended that information previously provided in the FTF PA and Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis that is unaffected by the new residual waste data be duplicated in this report.  The 
Special Analysis results will be used to inform decisions documented in Tanks 5 and 6 closure 
documents. 
2.0 
BASIS 
The Maintenance Guide for U.S. Department of Energy Low-Level Waste Disposal Facility 
Performance Assessments and Composite Analyses (DOE_11-10-1999) recognizes that conduct 
of a PA is not a static process, and states that, “Special analyses are expected to be needed as 
part of the routine maintenance of the performance assessment.”  As described in the 
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the 
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 16 of 160 
maintenance guide, “special analyses are analyses performed to evaluate the significance of new 
information or new analytical methods to the results of the performance assessment, or to 
supplement or amend the analyses performed in the original performance assessment.  A special 
analysis is not the same as a revision to the performance assessment, but the results of the 
special analysis may be used to determine whether a performance assessment revision is 
needed.”  As stated in the maintenance guide, a number of different factors may prompt a special 
analysis, including “wastes that exceed the concentrations analyzed for performance assessment-
significant radionuclides.”  
The guide also states that, “the purpose of conducting special analyses can be thought of as 
similar to the process for resolving unreviewed safety questions described in the DOE Order 
5480.21, Unreviewed Safety Questions.  The intent of the process is to provide flexibility in day-
to-day operations and to require those issues with a significant impact on the performance 
assessment’s conclusions, and therefore the projected compliance with performance objectives, 
to be brought to the proper level for attention.”  [DOE_11-10-1999] 
3.0 
TANKS 5 AND 6 BACKGROUND INFORMATION 
3.1 
Savannah River Site Characteristics 
The SRS is located in south-central South Carolina, approximately 100 miles from the Atlantic 
Coast.  The major physical feature at SRS is the Savannah River, approximately 20 miles of 
which serves as the southwestern boundary of the site and the South Carolina-Georgia border.  
The SRS encompasses portions of Aiken, Barnwell, and Allendale counties in South Carolina.  
The SRS occupies approximately 310 square miles, or more than 198,000 acres, and contains 
operations, service, and research and development areas.  The developed areas occupy less than 
10 % of the SRS footprint while the remainder of the site is undeveloped forest or wetlands.  
[SRS-REG-2007-00002]    
Additional site characteristics are addressed in Section 3.1 of FTF PA.  Topics addressed in 
Section 3.1 of FTF PA include geography, demography, meteorology, climatology, ecology, 
geology, seismology, hydrogeology, geochemistry, and natural resources. 
3.2 
F-Tank Farm Facility Description 
A legacy of the SRS mission was the generation of liquid waste from chemical separations 
processes in both F and H Areas.  Since the beginning of SRS operations, an integrated waste 
management system has evolved, which consists of several facilities designed for the overall 
processing of liquid waste.  Two of the major components of this system are the FTF and 
H-Tank Farm (HTF) (located in F Area and H Area, respectively), near the center of the site 
(Figure 3.2-1).  In F Area, plutonium, uranium, and other radionuclides were separated from 
target assemblies using chemical separations processes.  The tank farms, which store and process 
waste from the chemical separations operations, include waste tanks, evaporators, transfer line 
systems, and other ancillary structures.  Additional FTF facility characteristics are addressed in 
Section 3.2 of FTF PA. 
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the 
Revision 1

Page 18 of 160 
The FTF site was chosen because of its favorable terrain, proximity to the F-Canyon Separations 
Facility (the major waste generation source), and isolation distance from the SRS boundaries.  
Figure 3.2-1 shows the distance of the FTF from the SRS boundary.  Figure 3.2-2 shows the 
setting of F Area and FTF within the General Separations Area (GSA).   
Figure 3.2-2:  Layout of the GSA 
 
The FTF is a 22-acre site, which consists of 22 waste tanks and supporting ancillary structures.  
The major FTF ancillary structures are transfer lines, two evaporator systems, six diversion 
boxes (DBs), one catch tank, one concentrate transfer system (CTS) tank, and three pump pits 
(PPs).  There are three major waste tank types in FTF with nominal operating capacities ranging 
from 750,000 gallons (Type I tanks) to 1.3 million gallons (Type III, IIIA, and IV tanks).  
Figure 3.2-3 shows the general layout of FTF.  The waste tanks have varying degrees of 
secondary containment (liner) and in-tank structural features (e.g., cooling coils and columns).  
All FTF waste tank types have primary liners constructed of carbon steel.  The FTF was 
constructed to receive waste generated by various SRS production, processing, and laboratory 
facilities.  The use of FTF safely isolated these wastes from the environment, SRS workers, and 
the public.  The FTF PA provides extensive descriptions of the FTF and waste processing 
facilities.  [SRS-REG-2007-00002]

Figure 3.2-3:  General Layout of F-Tank Farm 
 
3.3 
Tanks 5 and 6 
Tanks 5 and 6 are Type I tanks.  There are eight Type I tanks in FTF (Tanks 1 through 8).  The 
FTF Type I tanks were constructed between 1951 and 1953 as part of the original site 
construction activities.  Figure 3.3-1 shows a typical Type I tank.  These waste tanks have a 
carbon-steel primary liner and a 5-foot high carbon-steel annulus pan (secondary liner) that 
forms an annular space 2.5 feet wide between the secondary and primary liners (see Figure 3.3-2 
and 3.3-3).  Type I tanks are 75 feet in diameter and 24.5 feet in height with a nominal operating 
capacity of 750,000 gallons.  Twelve concrete and steel columns support the roof of a Type I 
tank.  Type I tanks have 34 vertical cooling coil arrays and 2 horizontal cooling coil arrays that 
extend across the bottom of the waste tanks.  Section 3.2.1.1 of the FTF PA provides additional 
details of the Type I tanks.  [SRS-REG-2007-00002]

F-TANK FARM PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT BACKGROUND 
INFORMATION 
This Special Analysis is being prepared based on the information presented in the FTF PA and 
the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  The FTF PA was prepared to support the eventual operational 
closure of the FTF underground waste tanks and ancillary equipment.  The Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis evaluated the impact of the Tank 18 and Tank 19 final inventories and updated 
parameter sensitivity studies on the results and conclusion of the FTF PA.  The FTF PA provides 
the technical basis and results to be used in subsequent documents to demonstrate compliance 
with the pertinent requirements from the documents identified below for final closure of FTF as 
indicated in Table 4.0-1: 
 DOE M 435.1-1  
 NDAA Section 3116 
 Bureau of Water Permit to Construct, F and H-Area High-Level Radioactive Waste Tank 
Farms, Construction Permit #17,424-IW (DHEC_01-25-1993) 
 Federal Facility Agreement (FFA) for the Savannah River Site (WSRC-OS-94-42) 
The key requirements from the preceding documents necessitate development and calculation of 
the following for the FTF: potential radiological doses to a hypothetical MOP; potential 
radiological doses to a hypothetical inadvertent intruder; radiological dose to a human receptor 
via the air pathway, radon flux and water concentrations.  All of these calculations were 
performed to provide results over a minimum of 10,000 years.  The water concentrations were

In accordance with the FFA requirements for high-level radioactive waste tank system(s), a 
construction and operating permit (hereinafter referred to as Construction Permit #17,424-IW) 
was obtained for the SRS tank farms’ waste tank systems from the South Carolina Department of 
Health and Environmental Control (SCDHEC).  [DHEC_01-25-1993]  The FFA requires that 
waste tank system(s) that have been issued an industrial wastewater operating permit under the 
Pollution Control Act (PCA), shall be removed from service in accordance with S.C. Code Ann., 
Section 48-1-10, et seq. (1985) and all applicable regulations promulgated pursuant to the PCA.  
[Title 48_Chapter 1_SC_Laws]  Applicable regulations include SCDHEC Regulation 61-67, 
Standards for Wastewater Facility Construction and Regulation 61-82, Proper Closeout of 
Wastewater Treatment Facilities.  [WSRC-OS-94-42 Section IX.E (4)]  The SCDHEC has 
advised that this process will involve two bureaus (Bureau of Water and Bureau of Land and 
Waste Management).   
The regulatory process to complete closure of the FTF requires the development of multiple 
detailed technical documents with reviews and approvals by state and federal agencies.  The 
documents involved include DOE’s final revision of the FTF basis for Section 3116 
determination for facility closure, which will be used to demonstrate that the criteria in NDAA 
Section 3116 are met.  [NDAA_3116]  DOE’s Basis for Section 3116 Determination for Closure 
of F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site (hereinafter referred to as the FTF 3116 Basis 
Document) provides a basis upon which the Secretary of Energy (in consultation with the NRC) 
has determined that the criteria in NDAA Section 3116 is met and that the stabilized residual 
waste at closure is not high-level waste.  [DOE/SRS-WD-2012-001]  The criteria in NDAA 
Section 3116 provide that the waste will be disposed of in compliance with the performance 
objectives in 10 CFR Part 61, Subpart C.  The current revision of the FTF PA (SRS-REG-2007-
00002) provides the technical basis that has been used to demonstrate compliance with 10 CFR 
61.41 (Protection of the General Population from Releases of Radioactivity) and 61.42 
(Protection of Individuals from Inadvertent Intrusion) performance objectives, as described in 
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the 
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 24 of 160 
the FTF 3116 Basis Document.  [10 CFR 61]  These performance objectives are used in concert 
with the comparable performance objectives from DOE M 435.1-1.   
Compliance with the SCDHEC requirements will be demonstrated using two primary documents 
that are supported by FTF PA.  The first is the Industrial Wastewater General Closure Plan for 
F-Area Waste Tank Systems, Industrial Wastewater Construction Permit #17,424-IW (LWO-
RIP-2009-00009), which establishes the general protocols, requirements, and processes for 
operational closure of FTF.  The second set of documents are waste tank-specific closure 
modules that authorize the operational closure and grouting of a specific waste tank, group of 
waste tanks, or ancillary equipment.     
The FTF PA provides the technical information at different points of assessment that can be used 
in subsequent decision documents (e.g., waste tank-specific closure modules).  The FTF PA 
provides groundwater radionuclide concentrations at one meter, 100 meters, and exposure points 
at the two seeplines approximately 1,600 meters from FTF.  The groundwater concentrations are 
provided for each of the three aquifers as applicable as a part of the FTF groundwater modeling.  
FTF PA also provides groundwater concentrations for chemical contaminants at one meter and 
100 meters.  In addition to doses determined from these groundwater concentrations, the FTF PA 
provides inadvertent intruder doses consistent with the requirements for 10 CFR 61.42, as well as 
analyses for the air pathways and radon ground-surface flux, as required by DOE M 435.1-1.   
5.0 
TANKS 5 AND 6 RESIDUAL WASTE INFORMATION 
As part of the Tanks 5 and 6 operational closure process, actual residual inventories at 
operational closure have been determined for Tanks 5 and 6 using samples from the tanks after 
waste removal activities were completed.  The waste characterization methodology used to 
develop the Tanks 5 and 6 characterizations summed the inventory from discrete areas of each 
waste tank.  Each area’s residual inventory was determined by taking its material concentration 
and scaling it by the corresponding residual volume.  The calculation was repeated for each 
constituent (radionuclides and chemicals).  Tables 5.0-1 and 5.0-2 list Tanks 5 and 6 residual 
inventories at final facility closure for the FTF radionuclides and chemicals, respectively.  [SRR-
CWDA-2009-00045] 
Note that the combined closure inventories of Tc-99 from the Type I tanks (including Tanks 5 
and 6) were reduced from a projected 632 total curies to less than 8 total curies based on actual 
Tank 5 and Tank 6 characterization.  This reduction is important as Tc-99 releases from the Type 
I tanks contributed to a significant future dose peak in the FTF PA, but do not contribute 
significantly in this special analysis, as shown in Section 6.3.5.2.  [SRS-REG-2007-00002]

6.0 
F-TANK FARM PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT EVALUATION 
This section will evaluate the new information (i.e., final characterization) on Tanks 5 and 6 
residual wastes at operational closure presented in Section 5.0, as well as the impact that this new 
characterization has on the residual waste estimates for other waste tanks.  This new inventory 
information is used to update the FTF fate and transport modeling performed as part of FTF PA 
and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  The potential impacts of the new inventory information on 
the assumptions from the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis are also considered.  As 
part of this evaluation, the final Tanks 5 and 6 characterization data was used to update the 
inventory projection for all of the FTF Types I, III, and IIIA tanks.  [SRR-CWDA-2009-00045] 
6.1 
F-Tank Farm Facility Characteristics 
This section will discuss the impact of the new residual waste information on the FTF 
characteristics information presented in Section 3.0 of the FTF PA. 
6.1.1 Site Characteristics 
Section 3.1 of FTF PA contains a description of SRS site characteristics.  The site 
characteristics information presented in the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis are 
not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.1.2 Principal Facility Design Features 
Section 3.2 of FTF PA contains a description of the FTF facility design features.  The FTF 
facility design features information presented in the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis are not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.1.3 Stabilized Contaminant Characteristics 
Section 3.3 of FTF PA contains a description of the FTF stabilized contaminant 
characteristics, which is affected by the new residual waste information as detailed below.   
Section 3.3.1 of FTF PA describes the stabilized contaminant characterization screening 
process.  The screening process is not affected by the new residual waste information.    
Section 3.3.2 of FTF PA includes the individual waste tank inventories, which are an integral 
part of the modeling process.  The updated FTF waste tank radionuclide and chemical 
inventories are presented in Tables 6.1-1 and 6.1-2.  The font colors are provided to highlight 
differences in these inventories relative to those used in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  
Additional details regarding determination of the revised FTF waste tank projected 
inventories and inventories at operational closure (i.e., for Tanks 17 through 20 and Tanks 5 
and 6) can be found in the FTF inventory report (SRR-CWDA-2009-00045).

Page 34 of 160 
The impacts of these new FTF inventories on various PA analyses (e.g., public dose 
analyses) are presented later in Section 6.3 of this Special Analysis. 
Section 3.3.3 of FTF PA describes the FTF ancillary equipment inventory.  The ancillary 
equipment inventory is based on the residual inventories at those specific locations (e.g., 
evaporator vessels, pump tanks) and is not affected by the new residual waste information.   
6.2 
The F-Tank Farm Analysis of Performance 
This section will discuss the impact of the new residual waste information on the FTF analysis of 
performance information presented in Section 4.0 of the FTF PA and Section 6.2 of the Tank 
18/19 Special Analysis. 
6.2.1 Overview of Analysis 
Section 4.1 of FTF PA contains an overview of analysis.  The overview of analysis 
information presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.2.2 Integrated Site Conceptual Model of Facility Performance 
Section 4.2 of FTF PA describes the Integrated Conceptual Model (ICM), which is used to 
simulate the release of radiological and chemical contaminants from the 22 underground 
waste tanks and associated ancillary equipment in FTF.  The Section 4.2 ICM information 
presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste information.   
Section 4.2.2 (Source Term Release) of FTF PA includes a discussion of the waste release 
model, which is an integral part of the modeling process.  The information regarding the 
residual waste characteristics presented in the Tanks 5 and 6 waste characterization reports 
(SRR-CWDA-2012-00027, SRR-CWDA-2012-00075) is summarized in Section 5.0 of this 
Special Analysis, is consistent with the source term release assumptions, and does not impact 
the waste release model. 
6.2.3 Modeling Codes 
Section 4.3 of FTF PA contains a discussion of the modeling codes.  The FTF PA Base Case 
(i.e., deterministic) modeling was performed using the same flow and transport model that 
was used in the FTF PA, Rev. 1 and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  The FTF PORFLOW 
flow and transport model was not changed for the modeling runs performed for this Special 
Analysis but the model was rerun with the updated FTF inventories.  For the PORFLOW 
deterministic sensitivity runs, some additional inputs were updated, as discussed in detail in 
Section 6.3.6.   
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the  
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 35 of 160 
FTF probabilistic modeling for this Special Analysis was performed using the GoldSim 
model: SRS FTF v3.0.  This GoldSim model is an update of the model used for FTF PA and 
the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis with multiple changes and improvements, as detailed in the 
report F-Area Tank Farm Stochastic Fate and Transport Model (SRR-CWDA-2012-00119).  
SRS FTF v3.0 identified and corrected errors from the previous GoldSim model and an 
improved modeling structure was applied.  The updated FTF inventories were used in new 
the modeling runs.  [SRR-CWDA-2009-00045]  Quality assurance (e.g., verification of 
modeling inputs used) for this model revision is documented in the Tanks 5 and 6 Special 
Analysis for the Performance Assessment for the F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site: 
Quality Assurance Report (SRR-CWDA-2012-00134).  The modeling codes information 
presented in FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.2.4 Closure System Modeling 
Section 4.4 of FTF PA describes how the FTF design elements and their associated 
properties were represented in the computer modeling codes.  The closure system modeling 
information presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.2.5 Airborne and Radon Analysis 
Section 4.5 of FTF PA contains a discussion of the airborne and radon analysis methodology.  
The air and radon pathway conceptual model and analysis approach presented in the FTF PA 
is affected by the new residual waste information and a revised analysis methodology.  In 
Section 4.5 of the FTF PA, the airborne and radon pathway analysis conservatively assumed 
that the concrete roof, reducing grout, and stabilized contaminant layer were dry (i.e., 0 % 
saturation).  When applying the revised inventory data, this assumption proved to be overly 
conservative to the extent that the results were not realistic. 
An alternative assumption of 50 % saturation for the concrete roof, reducing grout, and 
stabilized contaminant layer was applied for this Special Analysis.  Given the short curing 
time and material water-retention properties of these materials, the modeled environment is 
expected to be at or near saturation.  [WSRC-STI-2007-00369]  Assuming 50 % saturation 
maintains conservatism, but more closely reflects expected conditions.  This revised 
methodology for determining airborne and radon pathway releases is consistent with the 
approach used for more recent PAs (e.g., HTF PA).  [SRR-CWDA-2010-00128]  
6.2.6 Biotic Pathways 
Section 4.6 of FTF PA documents the bioaccumulation factors and human health exposure 
parameters used in the FTF PA modeling effort.  The bioaccumulation factors and human 
health exposure parameters presented in the FTF PA are not affected by the new residual 
waste information. 
6.2.7 Dose Analysis 
Section 4.7 of FTF PA contains a discussion of the dose analysis approach used and presents 
the set of dose conversion factors (DCFs) used in the dose calculations modeling effort 
methodology.  The dose analysis information presented in FTF PA is not affected by the new 
residual waste information.

6.3 
FTF Results of Analysis 
This section will discuss the impact of the new residual waste information on the FTF results 
presented in Section 5.0 of FTF PA and Section 6.3 of the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis. 
6.3.1 Source Term (Analyses Results) 
Section 5.1 of FTF PA presents the peak stabilized contaminant release rates from the FTF 
waste tanks and ancillary equipment.  The release rates (fluxes) from PORFLOW were not 
presented herein, since the flows did not change and the groundwater concentrations at 100 
meters from the FTF boundary provide analogous information (Section 6.3.2.1). 
6.3.2 Environmental Transport of Radionuclides 
Section 5.2 (Environmental Transport of Radionuclides) of FTF PA presents the groundwater 
concentrations for the FTF radionuclides and chemicals.  Maximum groundwater 
concentrations are presented for two exposure points: 1) 100 meters from the FTF and 2) at 
the seeplines for Upper Three Runs (UTR) and Fourmile Branch.  Results are presented in 
FTF PA for the three distinct aquifers modeled, UTR Aquifer-Upper Zone (UTRA-UZ), 
UTRA-Lower Zone (UTRA-LZ), and Gordon Aquifer.  The groundwater concentrations at 
100 meters and at the seepline were recalculated using the PORFLOW FTF model for the 
FTF PA Base Case using the revised FTF inventories presented in Section 6.1.3 of this 
Special Analysis.   
6.3.2.1 
Groundwater Concentrations 
Groundwater concentrations at 100 meters are calculated in the FTF PA using the 
PORFLOW FTF model, which divides the area around FTF into computational cells.  
Calculation of the 100-meter groundwater concentrations using the PORFLOW FTF model is 
discussed in more detail in Section 5.2.2 of the FTF PA.  The PORFLOW 100-meter 
groundwater concentrations are calculated for five sectors (Sectors A through E) as shown on 
Figure 6.3-1.  The peak concentration values for the 100-meter results are recorded for the 
three aquifers of concern (i.e., UTRA-UZ, UTRA-LZ, and Gordon Aquifer).  The five 
sectors are analyzed for each radionuclide and chemical to find the maximum groundwater 
concentrations at 100 meters from the FTF.   
The PORFLOW one-meter concentrations are calculated for four sectors (Sector 1A through 
1D), as shown in Figure 6.3-1.  Using the highest groundwater concentration from any sector 
to determine the calculated peak doses overstates the dose results.  This is because the 
concentration for each radionuclide is determined independent of the location (i.e., horizontal 
mesh and aquifer) within the given sector.

Tables A-1 through A-3 (Appendix A) present the updated peak 100-meter radionuclide 
concentrations within 1,000 years in each sector for the three aquifers.  These radionuclide 
concentrations reflect the peak concentrations for each radionuclide in the sector.     
Tables A-1 through A-3 (Appendix A) also list the MCL for each constituent with the 
derived values for beta-gamma and photon emitters from Table II-3 of FR-00-9654.  The 
MCLs provided in the reference are based on a beta-gamma dose of 4 mrem/yr.  The peak 
concentration of each beta-gamma emitter is compared to a specific MCL to determine their 
fraction.  To determine if the 4 mrem/yr beta-gamma limit is met, the sum of the fractions 
must be less than 1.0.  The total alpha MCL includes Ra-226, but does not include radon or 
uranium.  The radium MCL includes both Ra-226 and Ra-228.  [SCDHEC R.61-58] 
Tables A-4 through A-6 (Appendix A) show the peak updated 100-meter chemical 
concentrations within 1,000 years in each sector for the three aquifers.  Similarly, Appendix 
B presents peak radionuclide concentrations within 10,000 years.

6.3.2.2 
Sensitivity Run Radionuclide Determination 
Section 5.2.2 of the FTF PA presents the methodology used to determine which 
radionuclides were most significant and to document which radionuclides would be 
considered a “sensitivity run radionuclide.”  While all radionuclides identified in the FTF 
waste tank inventory were included in 100-meter groundwater modeling efforts, narrowing 
the catalog of radionuclides down to a sensitivity run radionuclide list allowed the analysis to 
concentrate on the few radionuclides which posed more risk and concentrated modeling 
efforts on the areas of greatest concern.  Only the sensitivity run radionuclides were included 
in the PORFLOW seepline modeling runs, with doses associated with seepline 
concentrations for the other radionuclides calculated by using 5 % of the applicable 
100-meter groundwater concentrations.  The 5 % factor is based on the ratio calculated 
between the bounding concentrations for the sensitivity run radionuclides at 100 meters and 
the seepline. 
The sensitivity run radionuclides were recalculated based on the peak 100-meter groundwater 
concentrations determined using the revised FTF inventories.  Any radionuclide with a dose 
(assuming FTF PA Base Case pathways and assumptions) greater than 0.1 mrem/yr was 
considered a sensitivity run radionuclide.  The sensitivity run, radionuclide determination 
was conducted based on the peak 100-meter groundwater dose within 20,000 years.   
The new screening conclusions are provided in Table 6.3-1.  The resulting list of sensitivity 
run radionuclides (Am-241, Am-243, C-14, Cl-36, Cm-244, Cs-135, I-129, Nb-93m, Nb-94, 
Np-237, Pa-231, Pu-238, Pu-239, Pu-240, Pu-241, Ra-226, Tc-99, Th-230, U-234, U-235, 
U-238, and Zr-93) is different from the FTF PA, in that Th-229 and U-233 were deleted and 
Cl-36, Nb-93m, Nb-94, Pu-241, U-238, Zr-93 were added.  This revised set is intended to 
capture those radionuclides with the greatest potential to influence dose results, based on the 
revised inventories.

conservatively high for the radionuclides present in multiple decay chains because the totals

regard to time or location.  Similarly, Tables B-7 (UTR) and B-8 (Fourmile Branch) in

6.3.3 Air Pathway and Radon Analysis 
Section 5.3 of FTF PA presents the air pathway and radon analysis results.  Section 4.5 of 
FTF PA describes the method used to conservatively bound the dose from airborne 
radionuclides and the results in that section provides a dose to maximally exposed individual 
(MEI) per curie of inventory.  The approach described in the FTF PA assumed 0 % saturation 
of the concrete tank roofs, tank grout, and stabilized contaminant layers.  For this Special 
Analysis, an alternative assumption of 50 % saturation more closely reflects expected 
conditions.  This alternative assumption is consistent with more recent PAs (e.g., the HTF 
PA).  [SRR-CWDA-2010-00128]

The instantaneous radon flux is determined by multiplying the peak flux by the total 
inventory divided by the total area in the FTF.  Table 6.3-5 summarizes the total inventory of 
isotopes contributing to the radon flux.  The peak dose of radon for the period is assumed to 
be at 10,000 years.  These results are highly conservative because the entire inventory is 
assumed concentrated in a 1-foot layer in a Type I tank.  As shown in Table 6.3-6, the peak 
instantaneous radon flux using the entire FTF inventory (presented in Section 6.1.3) is 
1.6E-16 pCi/m2/s.

6.3.4 Biotic Pathways  
Section 5.4 of FTF PA describes how the biotic pathways doses to the MOP are calculated 
for the receptor with 100-meter well water as a primary water source and for the receptor 
with groundwater from a stream as a primary water source.  The information regarding biotic 
pathways calculations presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste 
information. 
6.3.5 Dose Analysis of MOP Exposure 
Section 5.5 (Dose Analysis) of FTF PA contains calculations of the peak total doses for a) 
the MOP at the 100-meter well and b) the MOP at applicable streams (either UTR or 
Fourmile Branch).  The peak doses have been recalculated using the peak groundwater 
concentrations identified in Section 6.3.2 of this Special Analysis (i.e., the groundwater 
concentrations at 100 meters and at the seepline).  The doses were recalculated using the 
PORFLOW FTF model for the FTF PA Base Case using the revised FTF inventories.   
6.3.5.1 
Member of the Public at 100-Meter Groundwater Pathway Dose Results 
Table 6.3-7 shows a comparison of the 100-meter peak groundwater pathway doses 
(recalculated using the concentration associated with the revised FTF inventories) for the 
different 100-meter sectors within 1,000 and 10,000 years.  In calculating the peak 
groundwater pathway dose, the highest radionuclide concentration within each sector’s 
vertical computational mesh from each of the three distinct aquifers modeled (UTRA-UZ, 
UTRA-LZ, and the Gordon Aquifer), is used.

Using the actual residual inventories at operational closure for Tanks 5, 6, 17, 18, 19, and 20, 
as well as the revised estimates for the other waste tanks, resulted in no significant change to 
the peak groundwater pathway doses in 10,000 years, relative to the Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis.  The peak groundwater pathway dose in 100,000 years increased relative to the 
Tank 18/19 Special Analysis, which reported a peak of approximately 500 mrem/yr at 
approximately 40,000 years.  The increased Zr-93 inventory projected in the Type I tanks 
resulted in an increased dose contribution from the Nb-93m (a decay product of Zr-93), thus 
increasing the peak 100,000-year dose to nearly 600 mrem/yr. 
This dose represents conservative modeling assumptions that were incorporated into the FTF 
PA Base due to uncertainty inherent in a number of the modeling parameters.  Since the 
development of the FTF PA Base Case, additional studies have been performed that have 
improved understanding of the waste release model and of specific parameters related to 
contaminant transport.  [SRNL-STI-2012-00404, SRNL-STI-2011-00011, SRNL-STI-2011-
00672, ML073510127]  This more recent data was incorporated into a Composite Sensitivity 
Study (see Section 6.3.6.3).  This Composite Sensitivity Study shows that the dose results 
from both Pu-239 and Nb-93m are conservative in the Base Case due to modeling 
assumptions.  The updated parameters result in a lower peak 100,000-year dose (with a peak 
of only about 20 mrem/yr), driven by Np-237 and Ra-226 releases.

6.3.5.2 
Individual Radionuclide Contributions to the MOP 100-Meter Peak Annual 
Groundwater Pathway Dose 
Figures 6.3-7 through 6.3-10 show the relative contributions from individual radionuclides to 
the Sector E 100-meter groundwater pathway dose over time.  Table 6.3-8 presents 
radionuclide contributions to the 3.3 mrem/yr3 peak groundwater pathway dose.  The peak 
groundwater pathway dose to the MOP at 100-meters in 10,000 years is primarily associated 
with Ra-226 (52 %), Np-237 (17 %), Pa-231 (15 %), and U-234 (13 %).  The individual 
contributions to the peak groundwater pathway dose in 10,000 years are essentially 
unchanged (relative to the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis) when the actual residual inventories 
at operational closure are used for Tanks 5, 6, 17, 18, 19, and 20.   
The increase to the peak dose in the first 10,000 years, relative to the FTF PA, associated 
with Ra-226 is due to the Pu-238 inventory at operational closure in Tank 18 (Pu-238 is a 
parent to Ra-226), which is higher than the projected inventory used in the FTF PA.  The 
peak dose in 10,000 years associated with Pa-231 is driven by the Pa-231 inventory at 
operational closure in Tank 18 differing from the projected inventory.   
As shown in Figure 6.3-9, the Tc-99 contribution to the peak dose is significantly less than it 
was in the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis due to the smaller Tc-99 inventory 
in the Type I waste tanks.  At approximately 18,000 years after closure (and continuing 
throughout the duration of the model), the Nb-93m released from the Type I waste tanks 
contributes 20 to 50 mrem/yr.  Additional sensitivity analyses were performed as part of this 
Special Analysis to better assess the potential impacts associated with Nb-93m (see Section 
6.3.6.3).  The Composite Sensitivity Study used more probable and defensible inputs, 
providing greater confidence that the FTF PA results and conclusions remain valid to 
demonstrate that the FTF PA Base Case incorporates conservative approaches/inputs related 
to the release and transport of Nb-93m and Pu-239.

6.3.5.3 
Individual Tank Contributions to MOP 100-Meter Peak Annual 
Groundwater Pathway Dose 
The following describes the impacts of the updated inventories for Tanks 5 and 6.  Although 
Sector E provides the highest doses when considering all of the sources within the FTF (see 
Section 6.3.5.1), Tanks 5 and 6 provide greater impacts on Sector D, therefore this discussion 
focuses on the Sector D dose results.  Since the liners for Tanks 5 and 6 are not projected to 
fail in the Base Case within 10,000 years, the contributors to peak dose within 10,000 years 
are unchanged from previous analyses.  [SRR-CWDA-2010-00124]  
Table 6.3-9 presents the relative contributions from those residual inventories (Tanks 5 and 
6) which will contribute to the Sector D 100-meter MOP groundwater pathway dose.  Tanks 
5 and 6 are contributors (approximately 28 % and 16 %, respectively) to the 100-meter peak 
groundwater-pathway dose in Sector D within 20,000 years.  Figures 6.3-11 and 6.3-12 show 
the Sector D 100-meter MOP peak groundwater-pathway dose associated with the Tank 5 
residual inventory for 20,000 years and 100,000 years, respectively.  Similarly, Figures 6.3-
13 and 6.3-14 show the Sector D 100-meter MOP peak groundwater-pathway dose 
associated with the Tank 6 residual inventory for 20,000 years and 100,000 years, 
respectively.  Since Tanks 5 and 6 are modeled as having liners that remain intact during the 
first 10,000 years after FTF closure, the 20,000-year and 100,000-year timeframes were 
chosen to better understand barrier behavior and to illustrate peak doses.

Page 57 of 160 
With respect to Tanks 5 and 6, the most significant peak dose within 100,000 years is 
associated with a combination of Pu-239 and Nb-93m from Tank 5.  Although the peak dose 
in Sector D occurs around 42,000 years after closure, this dose is primarily driven by Pu-239 
released from Tank 18.  The maximum dose contributions from Tanks 5 and 6 both occur 
later than this, as seen in Figures 6.3-12 and 6.3-14.  Contributions from Tank 5 peak at 71 
mrem/yr, approximately 54,000 years after closure, at which time it comprises about 30 % of 
the total dose.  Similarly, Tank 6 peaks at 34 mrem/yr at approximately 56,000 years, at 
which time it comprises about 15 % of the total dose. 
To better assess the risk and uncertainty associated with these dose peaks, DOE developed a 
Composite Sensitivity Study that incorporated more recent data pertaining to the release and 
transport of contaminants, including Pu-239 and Nb-93m (see Section 6.3.6.3).  Based on this 
Composite Sensitivity Study, the Base Case dose results from both Pu-239 and Nb-93m are 
artificially high due to modeling assumptions.  The Composite Sensitivity Study shows the 
total dose (from all contaminants) peaks around 20 mrem/yr near 18,000 years (i.e., remains 
below 25 mrem/yr for 100,000 years). 
6.3.5.4 
Individual Pathway Contributions to MOP 100-Meter Peak Annual 
Groundwater Pathway Dose 
As stated previously, the total peak groundwater-pathway dose results are the summation of 
the doses associated with all the individual 100-meter well pathways.  Table 6.3-10 shows 
the relative contributions from the individual groundwater pathways to the recalculated (i.e., 
using the updated tank inventories) Sector E 100-meter MOP receptor dose at 10,000 years 
(the year of the peak dose).  The primary contributors are water ingestion (65 % of peak 
dose) and vegetable ingestion (31 % of peak dose), with Ra-226 being the principal 
radionuclide contributor for all of the individual groundwater pathways.  The individual 
pathway contributions are not significantly impacted by using the actual residual inventories 
at operational closure for Tanks 5 and 6 as compared to the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.

6.3.6 Uncertainty and Sensitivity Analysis 
Section 5.6 of the FTF PA considers the effects of uncertainties in the conceptual models 
used and sensitivities in the parameters used in the mathematical models.  The uncertainty 
analyses and sensitivity analyses were primarily performed using a probabilistic model (i.e., 
the GoldSim FTF model), but some additional single parameter sensitivity analyses were 
performed through deterministic modeling using both the PORFLOW and GoldSim models. 
The probabilistic model varies multiple parameters simultaneously, so concurrent effects of 
changes in the model can be analyzed, and the potential impact of changes can be assessed.  
This assessment allows for identification of parameters that are only of significance when 
varied simultaneously with another parameter.  The deterministic model single parameter 
analysis provides a method to evaluate parametric effects in isolation, so the importance of 
the uncertainty around a parameter of concern can be more effectively evaluated.  Using both 
probabilistic and deterministic models for sensitivity analysis versus a single approach 
provides additional information concerning which parameters are of most importance to the 
FTF PA modeling.    
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the  
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 65 of 160 
In general, the uncertainty analyses and sensitivity analyses information presented in the FTF 
PA and the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis are not affected by the new residual waste 
information, and the uncertainty analyses and sensitivity analyses insights remain unaffected.  
The impacts on the GoldSim benchmarking, as discussed in Section 5.6.2 of FTF PA, are not 
presented herein but are documented in the FTF GoldSim Model report.  [SRR-CWDA-
2012-00119]  Note that the benchmarking efforts are not impacted by the changes to the 
Tank 5 and 6 inventories but are impacted by the improvements to the modeling 
methodology applied within GoldSim.  The updated version (Version 3.0) of the FTF 
GoldSim Model is based on the version used for the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  [SRR-
CWDA-2010-00124]  Several improvements were made to the model to allow for a more 
detailed analysis (SRR-CWDA-2012-00119).  With respect to the use of FTF PA parameters 
presented in Section 5.6.3 of FTF PA, the parameter impacted by the residual inventories at 
operational closure is the radiological inventory, discussed in the Section 5.6.3.2 of FTF PA.  
Most of the  Tanks 5 and 6 residual inventories are within the inventory distributions used in 
the probabilistic model (documented in the FTF PA Table 5.6-3, Inventory Multipliers), with 
the exceptions of Am-243 (Tank 6), Ni-59 (Tank 6), Ni-63 (Tank 6), Th-230 (Tanks 5 and 6), 
and Zr-93 (Tanks 5 and 6).   
The following sections describe some additional uncertainty analyses and sensitivity analyses 
activities that were performed utilizing the Tanks 5 and 6 residual inventories at operational 
closure. 
6.3.6.1 
Deterministic Model Barrier Analysis 
Section 5.6.7.3 of the FTF PA provided a comprehensive barrier analyses that identified 
barriers to waste migration and evaluated the capabilities of each barrier as understood from 
the results of the FTF PA.  The barrier analyses assessed the contribution of individual 
barriers (e.g., closure cap, grout, contamination zone, waste tank liner, and waste tank 
concrete) by comparing contaminant flux results under various barrier conditions.  No new 
barrier analyses were performed in support of this Special Analysis.   
6.3.6.2 
Deterministic Model Single Parameter Sensitivity Analysis 
The impact of input variability on the peak dose was further evaluated in this Special 
Analysis by performing several single parameter sensitivity analyses using the Base Case 
PORFLOW FTF model with changes to select parameters (a detailed discussion of the 
PORFLOW FTF model and the individual FTF PA Base Case parameters is provided in the 
FTF PA).   
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the  
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 66 of 160 
Additional inventory sensitivity analyses were performed to provide insight into the potential 
effects of removing additional waste material from Tanks 5 and 6.  These analyses compare 
dose results using the final FTF Tank 5 inventories to one-half of the final FTF Tank 5 
inventories and by comparing dose results using the final FTF Tank 6 inventories to one-half 
of the final FTF Tank 6 inventories.  Results are shown for the 100-meter MOP, peak 
groundwater-pathway dose within 20,000 years (Figures 6.3-24 through 6.3-27) and within 
100,000 years (Figures 6.3-28 through 6.3-31).   
This sensitivity study shows that the peak doses (primarily due to Nb-93m) can be reduced 
by reducing the total curies of Zr-93 (the parent radionuclide) in Tanks 5 and 6 and that this 
reduction approaches a linear function (except when the Nb-93m is overwhelmed by the dose 
contribution from Pu-239 around 50,000 to 60,000 years after closure).   
The conservatisms surrounding the Pu-239 release and their effects on the FTF doses are 
discussed in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  It states that “doses associated with Pu-239 
can be expected to occur later than currently reflected in the FTF PA Base Case as additional 
conservatisms are removed” and that the peak dose associated with Pu-239 occurs well 
beyond 10,000 years.  [SRR-CWDA-2010-00124]  Additionally, the Composite Sensitivity 
Study described in Section 6.3.6.3 further illustrates that the contributions from Nb-93m and 
Pu-239 may both be conservatively high due to base case modeling simplifications.

6.3.6.3 
Deterministic Model Composite Sensitivity Analyses 
To evaluate the impact of uncertainty surrounding multiple parameters simultaneously, 
several new PORFLOW sensitivity analyses were performed incorporating lessons learned 
regarding waste release modeling and distribution coefficients into the Base Case modeling.  
The following tables represent Composite Sensitivity Study inputs based on lessons learned 
from data made available since the FTF PA and Tank 18/19 Special Analysis were released 
and based on inputs from the NRC Monitoring Plan.  
Tables 6.3-18 and 6.3-19 provide updated solubility information presented in Evolution of 
Chemical Conditions and Estimated Solubility Controls on Radionuclides in the Residual 
Waste Layer During Post-Closure Aging of High-Level Waste Tanks (SRNL-STI-2012-
00404), which was prepared subsequent to submittal of the FTF PA and the Tank 18/19 
Special Analysis.  This updated solubility report incorporated, and built upon new 
information, garnered from the plutonium solubility study described in Section 6.3.6 of the 
Tank 18/19 Special Analysis.  [SRR-CWDA-2010-00124]  In addition, the number of pore 
volumes of reacting fluid required to reach each stage of grout degradation has been updated 
in SRNL-STI-2012-00404, and this new information is provided in Table 6.3-20.

The new data from Tables 6.3-18 through 6.3-21 were used to run a sensitivity study, for 
comparison to the FTF PA Base Case.  As shown in Figures 6.3-32 and 6.3-33, doses 
associated with the more realistic and probable modeling assumptions can be expected to be 
lower than currently reflected in the FTF PA Base Case deterministic model, with a peak 
dose in 100,000 years of only 20 mrem/yr.  This 20 mrem/yr dose is approximately 15 times 
lower than the FTF PA Base Case peak dose, approximately 25 times lower than the Tank 
18/19 Special Analysis Base Case peak dose, and approximately 30 times lower than the 
Tanks 5 and 6 SA Base Case peak dose.

Special Analysis, but not in this Tank 5/6 Special Analysis. 
b. Applies Base Case assumptions for the waste release model and contaminant properties.  
c. 
Applies more realistic and probable assumptions for the waste release model and contaminant properties.

Figure 6.3-34 shows the individual dose contributors to the Sector E groundwater pathway 
dose for this Composite Sensitivity Study.  The Np-237 dose is associated with the timing of 
waste tank liner failures and subsequent chemical transitions of cementitious materials.  
Specifically, the solubility control associated with fill grout transitions results in a relatively 
acute peak dose shortly after Np-237 is released from the waste tank basemat.  The transition 
from reducing to oxidizing is a change of two orders of magnitude (from 1E-09 mol/L to 3E-
07 mol/L).  The transition from Region II to Region III is a change of another order of 
magnitude (from 3E-07 mol/L to 2E-06 mol/L).  The earlier Np-237 release is associated 
with the Type I waste tanks which have a thinner basemat (30 inches) than the Type III/IIIA 
waste tanks (41 to 42 inches).

A probabilistic uncertainty analysis has been performed (using the GoldSim FTF model 
version SRS FTF v3.0) to assess the impact of the revised FTF inventory distributions.  In 
addition to the revised inventories and inventory distributions, this probabilistic model 
incorporates other improvements to the GoldSim FTF model from the FTF PA as discussed 
in the GoldSim FTF v3.0 model report (SRR-CWDA-2012-00119).  This model was run 
twice, each for 1,000 realizations (for an All Cases probabilistic model and for the Base Case 
probabilistic model) to demonstrate the effects of the revised inventory data and modeling 
corrections.  Additionally, a Composite Sensitivity Study was performed to incorporate 
revised waste release model information and revised Kd values.  The results of these 
modeling runs are discussed below.

The probabilistic peak dose results (using the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 model) for 1,000 
All Cases realizations in 10,000 years showed the peak of the mean to be 106 mrem/yr 
and the 95th percentile of the doses to be 346 mrem/yr.  (In the FTF PA, the peak of the 
mean was 14 mrem/yr and the 95th percentile was 77 mrem/yr; and in the Tank 18/19 
Special Analysis the peak of the mean was 13 mrem/yr and the 95th percentile was 74 
mrem/yr).  In 100,000 years the peak of the mean was 549 mrem/yr (versus 344 mrem/yr 
in the FTF PA and 344 mrem/yr in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis) and the 95th 
percentile of the doses to be 1,969 mrem/yr (versus 1,919 mrem/yr in the FTF PA and 
1,554 mrem/yr in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis).   
The 10,000-year dose results are significantly higher than those shown previously (i.e., in 
the FTF PA and in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis) because 1) earlier releases of Pu-239 
and 2) increased inventories in some of the dose-significant radionuclides (such as Zr-93, 
which decays in Nb-93m).  Both of these factors are addressed by the Composite 
Sensitivity Study, which includes more probable and defensible assumptions regarding 
Pu-239 and Zr-93 release (see Section 6.3.6.3) than were assumed in the Base Case 
probabilistic model (described in Section 6.3.6.4.2). 
6.3.6.4.2 
Base Case Probabilistic Model 
Statistical data associated with the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 probabilistic peak dose results 
for 1,000 Base Case realizations is shown in Figure 6.3-39  (10,000 years linear scale), 
Figure 6.3-40  (10,000 years log scale), Figure 6.3-41 (100,000 years linear scale), and 
Figure 6.3-42  (100,000 years log scale). 
The probabilistic peak dose results (using the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 model) for 1,000 
Base Case realizations in 10,000 years showed the peak of the mean to be 11.5 mrem/yr 
(versus 4.8 mrem/yr in the FTF PA and 4 mrem/yr in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis).  
The primary reason for the higher 10,000-year dose is that, in some realizations, Pu-239 
became available for earlier release in the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 model than was 
modeled in the FTF PA.  The 95th percentile of the doses was 61 mrem/yr (versus 
30 mrem/yr in the FTF PA and 35 mrem/yr in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis).  In 
100,000 years, the peak of the mean was shown to be 542 mrem/yr and the 95th percentile 
of the doses to be 2,179 mrem/yr.  Note that Base Case doses were not calculated out to 
100,000 years in the FTF PA or the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis. 
The fact that the 95th percentile value is higher than calculated previously is not 
unexpected.  Although many of the reasonably conservative stochastic inventory 
distributions used in the FTF PA were tightened (as shown in Table 6.3-22), when the 
actual waste tank residual characterization data was used the Zr-93 inventory was 
substantially increased, leading to an increase in the dose associated with Nb-93m.

6.3.6.4.3 
Composite Sensitivity Study, All Cases Probabilistic Model 
New studies have increased DOE’s understanding of the physical and chemical 
parameters that affect PA results.  A final probabilistic run was performed that applied a 
revised waste release model (including updated solubility controls) and updated Kd 
values, in addition to the updated inventory parameters as described in Section 6.3.6.3.  
This Composite Sensitivity Study was run for 1,000 realizations, using the All Cases 
modeling configuration. 
Statistical data associated with the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 probabilistic peak dose results 
for 1,000 Composite Sensitivity Study realizations is shown in Figure 6.3-43 (10,000 
years linear scale), Figure 6.3-44 (10,000 years log scale), Figure 6.3-45 (100,000 years 
linear scale), and Figure 6.3-46 (100,000 years log scale). 
The probabilistic peak dose results (using the GoldSim SRS FTF v3.0 model) for 1,000 
All Cases realizations in 10,000 years showed the peak of the mean to be 2.5 mrem/yr 
and the 95th percentile of the doses to be 10.7 mrem/yr.  In 100,000 years, the peak of the 
mean was shown to be 27.8 mrem/yr and the 95th percentile of the doses to be 90.7 
mrem/yr.  As previously described, the peak of the mean in the FTF PA was 14 mrem/yr 
and the 95th percentile was 77 mrem/yr, and in the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis the peak 
of the mean was 13 mrem/yr and the 95th percentile was 74 mrem/yr. 
This composite analysis used input parameters that are less conservative but reflect 
assumptions that are more reasonable, relative to the modeling case discussed in Section 
6.3.6.4.1.  The most notable differences in the results were the reductions to the Nb-93m 
and Pu-239, which were driven by the application of more reasonable solubility controls 
and Kd values.  As an example, Nb-93m (and the parent Zr-93) was previously modeled 
without solubility controls and without Kd values.  This conservative assumption was 
used in the FTF PA, as the FTF PA inventory had underestimated the presence of these 
radionuclides, such that the dose contribution was not significant.  However, given the 
increased inventory, a more reasonable modeling approach was needed to assess the 
impact of this inventory appropriately.

The other modeling parameters described in the FTF PA Section 5.6.3 are unaffected by

6.3.7 RCRA/CERCLA Risk Analysis  
The RCRA/CERCLA risk assessment for the FTF final facility closure follows the current 
Area Completion Project protocols for human health and ecological risk assessments.  The 
FTF Contaminant Migration Constituents of Concern (CMCOC) were identified through a 
system that is consistent with both the Area Completion Project protocols and FTF PA.  The 
CMCOC were identified by modeling the release of contaminants and their travel through the 
vadose zone, using the deterministic Base Case model with the updated inventories.  The 
concentrations of contaminants that are modeled to reach the water table are compared to 
MCL, RSLs, PRGs, or other appropriate standards in cases where the constituent does not 
have an MCL.  Any constituents that are predicted to exceed these standards (i.e., fraction 
greater than 1.0) in the groundwater directly beneath FTF (one meter from boundary) are 
identified as CMCOC as shown in Tables 6.3-23 and 6.3-24, which reflects concentrations 
calculated using the revised FTF inventories.  Based on the 1,000-year concentration curves, 
the CMCOC identified using the described protocols are Np-237 and Tc-99.

6.3.7.1 
Neptunium-237 
Neptunium-237 does not have an MCL so the peak concentration of 1.6 pCi/L at 1 meter is 
compared to the calculated PRG of 7.7E-01 pCi/L.  This peak concentration occurs 722 years 
following FTF facility closure.  As shown in Table 6.3-25, the peak concentration drops to 
0.42 pCi/L at 100 meters, which is below the PRG value. 
6.3.7.2 
Technetium-99 
Technetium-99 has a peak concentration of 1,100 pCi/L at 1 meter compared to the MCL of 
900 pCi/L.  This peak concentration occurs approximately 678 years following FTF facility 
closure.  As shown in Table 6.3-25, the peak concentration drops to 270 pCi/L at 100 meters, 
which is below the MCL value.

6.3.8 ALARA Analysis 
Section 5.8 of the FTF PA describes how the “as low as reasonably achievable” (ALARA) 
requirement of DOE O 435.1, Chg. 1 and 10 CFR 61.41 are implemented for FTF.  The 
ALARA information presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste 
information. 
6.4 
FTF Inadvertent Intruder Analysis 
This section will discuss the impact of the new residual waste information on the FTF 
inadvertent intruder analysis information presented in Section 6.0 of the FTF PA. 
6.4.1 Groundwater Concentrations at One Meter 
Section 6.1 of the FTF PA presents the one-meter groundwater concentrations for the FTF 
radionuclides and chemicals.  Maximum groundwater concentrations are given for the 
modeling cell adjoining the analyzed source terms.  Results are presented for the three 
distinct aquifers modeled (UTRA-UZ, UTRA-LZ, and Gordon Aquifer).   
These groundwater concentrations were recalculated using the PORFLOW FTF model for 
the FTF PA Base Case using the revised FTF residual inventories presented in Section 6.3.1.  
Tables A-9 through A-11 (see Appendix A) show peak one-meter radionuclides 
concentrations for the three aquifers in 1,000 years.  These radionuclide concentrations 
reflect the peak concentrations for each radionuclide in the highest sector.  The peak 1-meter 
chemical concentrations for the three aquifers in 1,000 years are shown Tables A-12 through 
A-14 (see Appendix A).  These chemical concentrations also reflect the peak concentrations 
for the sector.  Similarly, Appendix B presents peak radionuclide concentrations within 
10,000 years.      
6.4.2 Acute Exposure Scenarios 
Section 6.2 of the FTF PA describes how the biotic pathways doses are calculated for the 
Acute Exposure Scenarios.  The acute exposure scenarios information presented in the FTF 
PA is not significantly affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.4.3 Chronic Exposure Scenarios 
Section 6.3 of FTF PA describes how the biotic pathways doses are calculated for the chronic 
exposure scenarios.  The chronic exposure scenarios information presented in the FTF PA is 
not affected by the new residual waste information. 
6.4.4 
Groundwater Doses at One Meter 
Section 6.4 of FTF PA contains calculations of the peak total intruder doses for the acute 
intruder scenario and for the chronic intruder agricultural (post-drilling) scenario.  For the 
acute intruder, doses were calculated assuming the acute intruder drills into a three-inch 
diameter transfer line at any time after the 100-year period of institutional control following 
FTF facility closure.  For the chronic intruder, annual doses were calculated assuming 
contamination from the drill cuttings, as well as from the use of water obtained from a well. 
Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis for the 
SRR-CWDA-2012-00106 
Performance Assessment for the  
Revision 1 
F-Tank Farm at the Savannah River Site 
January 2013 
 
 
 
 
Page 94 of 160 
As described in Section 6.5.2.2 of the FTF PA, “the waste tank engineered barriers (e.g., 
closure cap erosion barrier, tank top concrete, and tank liner, where applicable), will prevent 
drilling into the waste inventory.”  [SRS-REG-2007-00002]  Therefore, the acute intruder 
scenario is dependent on the transfer line inventory (i.e., it does not include a groundwater 
contribution) and is not affected by a revision to the FTF inventories.  The inadvertent human 
intruder doses were negligibly affected by the correction to the timing input in the soil 
buildup calculation.  The peak dose for the acute intruder in 10,000 years therefore remains 2 
millirem at year 100, primarily due to exposure to drill cuttings.   
The peak chronic doses have been recalculated using the peak groundwater concentrations 
identified in Section 6.3.2 of this Special Analysis (i.e., groundwater concentrations at 
1-meter and at the seepline recalculated using the PORFLOW FTF model for the FTF PA 
Base Case using the revised FTF inventories).   
For the chronic intruder, the contributions to the peak doses from the four 1-meter sectors 
were recalculated using the highest concentration for each radionuclide in the sector, with the 
recalculation based on the revised FTF inventories.  These peak doses were the total dose 
associated with drill cuttings and all the individual 1-meter well pathways.  Figure 6.4-1 
graphically presents the annual dose to the chronic intruder for each of the four one-meter 
sectors for 10,000 years after FTF facility closure.  As shown in Figure 6.4-1, the dose to the 
chronic intruder, within 10,000 years, is highest at 100 years after FTF final facility closure 
and is attributed to the earliest time after FTF final facility closure that an intruder is assumed 
to drill into the closure area.

Table 6.4-1 presents the chronic intruder peak dose within 10,000 years and identifies the 
contribution from the significant pathways and their contributing radionuclides.  The peak 
dose for the chronic intruder scenario in 10,000 years was 70 mrem/yr at year 100.  This peak 
annual dose was almost entirely due to ingestion of vegetables contaminated with drill 
cuttings, with 96 % of the 70 mrem/yr being due to vegetable ingestion.  The principal 
radionuclide contributors to this vegetable dose were the short-lived isotopes Sr-90/Y-90 and 
Cs-137/Ba-137m.   
The chronic intruder-scenario peak dose at year 100 does not include a groundwater 
contribution because no liner failures are assumed to occur until approximately 500 years 
after facility closure (which are associated with the ancillary equipment and transfer lines).  
Thus, the peak dose to the chronic intruder in 10,000 years does not vary by FTF sector.  
Therefore, the chronic intruder peak dose is not impacted by using the actual residual 
inventories at operational closure for Tanks 5 and 6.

As illustrated in Figure 6.4-2, the early peak dose to the chronic intruder (70 mrem/yr at year

to 90 mrem/yr by year 20,000.  This radionuclide is a decay product from an initial Zr-93 
inventory in Type I tanks.  Prior to inventory updates, the groundwater pathway contribution 
to the peak dose was approximately 73 mrem/yr in FTF PA (i.e., the inventory updates had 
no significant impact on the chronic intruder peak dose).

6.4.5 Inadvertent Intruder Uncertainty/Sensitivity Analysis 
Section 6.5 of the FTF PA considers the effects on the inadvertent intruder analysis of 
uncertainties in the conceptual models used and sensitivities to the parameters used in the 
mathematical models.  In general, the inadvertent intruder uncertainty and sensitivity 
analyses information presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual waste 
information and the uncertainty and sensitivity analyses insights remain unaffected.  The FTF 
PA sensitivity analysis most affected is the evaluation performed to investigate the effect of 
an intruder drilling into a waste tank (which is not considered a credible scenario).   
Given the influence of the inventories from Tanks 5 and 6 on estimating other waste tank 
inventories, the uncertainty ranges applied to the inventory estimates were updated for this 
Special Analysis. 
As described in the FTF PA, the chronic intruder dose is based on releases from FTF waste 
tanks using the same parameters as in the MOP analysis thus uncertainty analyses performed 
for the MOP, as related to tank releases, is also applicable to the chronic IHI.  In the FTF PA, 
the peak of the mean from the chronic IHI dose (within the first 20,000-years) is 
639 mrem/yr, with a median dose of 112 mrem/yr.  [SRS-REG-2007-00002]  The Base Case 
probabilistic model for the Tanks 5 and 6 Special Analysis (described in Section 6.3.6.4.2) 
showed chronic IHI doses with a peak of the mean of 965 mrem/yr, with a median dose of 
73 mrem/yr (within the first 20,000 years of the model).  The higher mean dose is associated 
with increased Pu-239 from Tank 18 and increased Zr-93 decaying into Nb-93m from the 
Type I tanks; whereas the lower median dose is associated with the reduced uncertainty in 
the inventory estimates.

6.5 
F-Tank Farm Interpretation of Results 
Section 7.0 of FTF PA summarizes the conservatisms used in modeling and provides a summary 
and interpretation of the results presented in Section 5.0 and Section 6.0 of the FTF PA.  The 
FTF PA conservatisms information presented in the FTF PA is not affected by the new residual 
waste information.  The integrated system behavior discussion provided in the Section 7.1.1 of 
FTF PA remains valid irrespective of the revised Tanks 5 and 6 residual inventories.  The 
individual dose results provided in the Section 7.1.2 of FTF PA and in Section 6.3 of the Tank 
18/19 Special Analysis have been updated based on the revised Base Case modeling using the 
updated FTF residual inventories at closure, and are summarized in the following sections.  An 
additional sensitivity study was performed to incorporate a revised waste release model and 
updated transport parameters. 
6.5.1 100-Meter (Water from Well) Groundwater Pathways Doses 
The peak 100-meter groundwater pathway doses in 1,000 years is in Sector E (0.4 mrem/yr) 
and Sector D (0.3 mrem/yr).  Similarly, in 10,000 years the peak 100-meter groundwater 
pathway doses are also in Sector E (3.3 mrem/yr5) and Sector D (2 mrem/yr), as expected, 
because these sectors are closest to the Type IV tanks, which are the only waste tanks 
projected in the Base Case to have their liner fail in less than 10,000 years.  The primary 
pathway contributors to the peak 100-meter groundwater dose are water ingestion and 
vegetable ingestion.  The 100-meter groundwater-pathway peak doses in 10,000 years are 
primarily associated with Ra-226, Pa-231, and Np-237.  The peak 100-meter groundwater 
pathway doses within 100,000 years are also in Sectors D and E.  The peak 100-meter 
groundwater pathway dose in 10,000 years increased relative to FTF PA when the actual 
residual inventories at facility closure are used for Tanks 5, 6, 17, 18, 19, and 20.  The 
increase in peak dose in 10,000 years is associated with Ra-226 (increased due to an increase 
in the Pu-238 inventory in Tank 18) and Pa-231 (increased due to an increase in the Pa-231 
inventory in Tank 18). 
6.5.2 Water at the Stream Groundwater Pathways Doses 
The peak-groundwater pathway dose at the stream in 10,000 years is associated with UTR.  
The MOP at the stream peak-groundwater pathway dose in 10,000 years is 0.1 mrem/yr at 
year 10,000.  The primary contributor to the UTR peak dose is water ingestion, followed by 
fish ingestion. 
6.5.3 All-Pathways Dose 
The peak all-pathways annual dose for the MOP at 100 meters is calculated using the highest 
100-meter groundwater-pathway dose results in 10,000 years in combination with the air 
pathway results.  The peak all-pathways annual dose for the MOP is 3.3 mrem/yr and is 
associated with Sector E at 100 meters.  The all-pathways dose was dominated by the 
groundwater pathway, with the airborne pathway adding an additional 5E-06 mrem/yr to the 
MOP.  When the actual residual inventories at facility closure are used for Tanks 5, 6, 17, 18,
