19, and 20, the peak all-pathways annual dose for the MOP at 100 meters increased relative 
to the Tank 18/19 Special Analysis from 3.2 mrem/yr to 3.3 mrem/yr6.   
The peak all-pathways dose was 0.4 mrem/yr in 1,000 years (at approximately year 700) and 
600 mrem/yr in 100,000 years (at year 43,000). 
6.5.4 Inadvertent Intruder Dose 
The peak dose for the acute intruder in 10,000 years is 2 millirem (primarily due to exposure 
to drill cuttings).  The acute intruder scenario does not include a groundwater contribution 
and therefore does not vary by FTF sector.  The peak dose for the chronic intruder scenario in 
10,000 years is approximately 70 mrem/yr.  This peak dose was almost entirely due to 
ingestion of vegetables contaminated with drill cuttings.  The chronic intruder scenario peak 
dose is also driven by the drill cutting contributions.  The peak inadvertent intruder doses in 
10,000 years were not significantly impacted when the actual residual inventories at facility 
closure are used for Tanks 5 and 6 and are not changed from FTF PA or Tank 18/19 Special 
Analysis. 
6.5.5 Airborne Dose / Radon Flux 
The annual dose from airborne releases resulted in a total dose 5E-06 mrem/yr (principally 
from C-14) at 100 meters from the FTF in 10,000 years.  These results were very 
conservative because the flux rates are based on simplified models.  These simplified models 
also resulted in a peak flux of radon at the ground surface of 2E-16 pCi/m2/s.   
6.5.6 Composite Sensitivity Study 
Recent laboratory studies and literature reviews have provided improved understanding of a 
number of important input parameters affecting the FTF PA.  Specifically, the waste release 
model was updated, as were radionuclide-specific transport values such as Kds.  A Composite 
Sensitivity Study applied this new information and combined it with the updated inventory 
data to estimate potential doses, showing a peak dose in 100,000 years of 20 mrem/yr.  In 
addition, the Composite Sensitivity Study assumptions were also applied to the probabilistic 
model, resulting in mean doses of less than 3 mrem/yr over 10,000 years and about 
28 mrem/yr over 100,000 years.  The composite sensitivity studies use more probable and 
defensible inputs, providing greater confidence that the results and conclusions remain valid.   
6.6 
FTF Performance Evaluation 
Section 8.0 of the FTF PA describes intended use and future work to be done to support its 
maintenance.  The FTF PA use and future work information presented in the FTF PA are not 
adversely impacted by the new residual waste information, and the new information documented 
in this Special Analysis will be used for further information during future FTF PA maintenance 
activities.

As mentioned in Section 6.2.3, the GoldSim FTF model (SRS FTF v3.0) incorporates multiple 
changes and improvements.  The intent of these changes was to simulate transport processes 
more accurately.  This model was used for benchmarking and probabilistic simulation (see 
Section 6.3.6.4), and included major refinements, as detailed in the report for the FTF GoldSim 
model v3.0.  [SRR-CWDA-2012-00119] 
7.0 
CONCLUSION 
The FTF PA provides groundwater radionuclide concentrations at one meter, 100 meters, and 
exposure points at the two seeplines approximately 1,600 meters from FTF.  The groundwater 
concentrations are provided for each of the three aquifers as applicable as a part of the FTF 
groundwater modeling.  The FTF PA also provides groundwater concentrations for chemical 
contaminants at one meter and 100 meters.  In addition, FTF PA provides intruder doses as well 
as analyses for the air pathways and radon ground surface flux.  The FTF PA results can be used 
in subsequent documents to demonstrate compliance with the pertinent requirements identified 
below for final facility closure of FTF as indicated in Table 7.0-1.

The FTF PA, Rev.1 peak intruder dose is 73 mrem/yr at year 101 from a chronic scenario, drilling through

flux is decreased to 1.6E-16 pCi/m2/s. 
Note 
The decrease in the air pathway dose is due to using a less conservative and more probable assumption of 
50 % saturation in the transport media (i.e., the concrete tank roofs, waste tank grout, and stabilized 
contaminant layers), as opposed to the 0 % saturation assumed in the FTF PA.   
The peak groundwater radionuclide concentrations were calculated and only Np-237 and Tc-99 
were above the respective PRG or MCL values at one meter; no PRG or MCL values were 
exceeded at 100 meters or at the seepline.  The peak concentrations for the chemicals of concern 
were also calculated, and all were less than the MCL or RSL at a distance of 1 meter from FTF.   
The Special Analysis results provide reasonable assurance that compliance is maintained with 
the specific requirements of NDAA Section 3116, DOE M 435.1-1, and the MCLs.  The 
additional sensitivity and uncertainty analyses performed using the lessons learned regarding 
waste release modeling and distribution coefficients provide further confidence.  The results 
presented in the FTF PA reflecting Tanks 5 and 6 final operational closure inventories are not 
significantly impacted by new information regarding the final residual inventories that are 
planned to be grouted in-place in Tanks 5 and 6.

Page 158 of 160 
 
APPENDIX C 
BETA-GAMMA PEAK CONCENTRATIONS RATIOED TO MCLS FOR 
INDIVIDUAL CONSTITUENTS RELATED BY TIMING AT THE 
100-METER BOUNDARY 
 
Appendix B Tables B-1 and B-2 provide a summary of beta-gamma MCL fractions summed by 
peak concentration in 10,000 years, regardless of timing.  This approach provides conservative 
results as not all constituents peak at the same time within 10,000 years.  Five radionuclides 
make up approximately 99 % of the beta-gamma inputs for the individual aquifers and sectors 
(C-14, I-129, Nb-93m, Ni-59, and Tc-99) within 10,000 years.   
For this appendix, the peak concentrations for these five radionuclides were analyzed 
individually by time of occurrence against the concentration at the same time for the remaining 
radionuclides.  For example, in UTRA-UZ Sector D, C-14 peaked in 10,000 years at 4,172 years.  
The remaining four radionuclides did not peak in that year, so the beta-gamma value at 4,172 
years for each of these remaining radionuclides was added to the beta-gamma value for C-14 to 
produce an estimated beta-gamma value for the year 4,172, resulting in a beta-gamma MCL 
fraction of 41 % for the UTRA-UZ Sector D (Table C-1).   
Tables C-1 and C-2 present the beta-gamma peak concentrations in 10,000 years by aquifer ratio 
to MCLs for the five radionuclides that comprise the majority of the total beta-gamma 
contribution utilizing concurrent time for Sectors D and E, respectively.
